S0001	I am a student.		nēnu vidyārthini.	నేను విద్యార్థిని.			src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0002	We are students.		manaṁ vidyārthulamu.	మనం విద్యార్థులము.		colloquially often -am	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0003	"""You are a teacher"" (to a teacher) [respectful]"		mīru ṭīchar.	మీరు టీచర్.		formal → mīru, no marker	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0004	"""You are a student"" (to a student) [informal]"		nuvvu vidyārthivi.	నువ్వు విద్యార్థివి.		informal → nuvvu + marker -vi	src::site reg::informal known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0006	Give the near partner for āviḍa, atanu, and avi.		īviḍa, itanu, ivi.	ఈవిడ, ఇతను, ఇవి.		keep the same meaning/register; change distance	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::001

S0024	"""I am an Indian"" (male speaker)."		nēnu bhāratīyuḍini.	నేను భారతీయుడిని.		male · -ḍu → -ḍi before -ni	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::001

S0026	"How do you say ""my mother""?"		mā amma.	మా అమ్మ.		"literally ""our mother"" — never nā amma"	src::site known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::001
S0027	"How do you say ""my father""?"		mā nānna.	మా నాన్న.		family unit → mā	src::site known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::001
S0028	"How do you say ""my friend"" when it's a boy? A girl?"		nā snēhituḍu. / nā snēhiturālu.	నా స్నేహితుడు. / నా స్నేహితురాలు.		or just nā friend	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::question set::001
S0029	"""My husband"" — everyday and traditional."		nā bharta. / mā āyana.	నా భర్త. / మా ఆయన.		"""our respected he"""	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::001
S0030	"""My father is a teacher."""		mā nānna oka ṭīchar.	మా నాన్న ఒక టీచర్.		mā for family, loanword profession	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::001

S0036	Someone assumes you're a teacher. You're not. [respectful]		kādaṇḍi	కాదండి		negating an identity statement = kādu	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0037	Someone asks if there's any coffee left. There isn't. [respectful]		lēdaṇḍi	లేదండి		existence = lēdu	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0038	Sorry (lit. forgive me) [respectful]; Excuse me; Pardon me		kṣaminchaṇḍi	క్షమించండి			src::site src::book1000 reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 set::001
S0039	It was okay. / Not bad. / It's fine, don't worry about it.; Never mind		parvālēdu	పర్వాలేదు		"Enormously versatile. It also means ""no problem"" when someone apologises, and ""it doesn't matter"" when someone offers something you're declining. Card this one today."	src::site src::book1000 known::100 len::1-3 set::001

S0050	"""How are you?"" — ask it respectfully, then answer."		mīru bāgunnārā? / nēnu bāgunnānu.	మీరు బాగున్నారా? / నేను బాగున్నాను.		both pronouns optional	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::4-6 set::001
S0051	Your mother's elder sister.		peddamma.	పెద్దమ్మ.		"pedda + amma — ""elder mother"""	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::001

S0053	Your father's younger brother.		chinnānna.	చిన్నాన్న.		"chinna + nāna — ""younger father"""	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0054	Your father's sister — and does her age matter?		atta.	అత్త.		No — age-neutral, like māmayya on the other side.	src::site known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002

S0056	Address your grandfather respectfully.		tāta gāru.	తాత గారు.		gāru attaches to people, aṇḍi to phrases	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0057	Your mother-in-law and father-in-law. [respectful]		atta gāru / māma gāru.	అత్త గారు / మామ గారు.		Same roots as the paternal aunt and maternal uncle.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::4-6 set::002
S0058	Son-in-law and daughter-in-law.		alluḍu / kōḍalu.	అల్లుడు / కోడలు.			src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0059	"""I am the son-in-law."" — mind the Lesson 2 sound rule."		nēnu alluḍini.	నేను అల్లుడిని.		-ḍu → -ḍi before the person marker	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0060	Ask a stranger their name.; What’s your name? (formal)		mī pēru ēmiṭi?	మీ పేరు ఏమిటి?		respectful · stranger → always mī, never nī	src::site src::anki reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002
S0061	Say that question the way it actually sounds at speed. [respectful]		mī pēreṇṭi?	మీ పేరెంటి?		pēru + ēnti — the vowel gets deleted	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002
S0062	"""His name"" using the formal pronoun."		āyana pēru.	ఆయన పేరు.		unchanged	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0063	"""Their names"" for a group of people."		vāḷḷa pērlu.	వాళ్ళ పేర్లు.		vāḷḷu → vāḷḷa, and pēru → pērlu	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0064	"""Our names"" — you're telling someone outside your group."		mā pērlu.	మా పేర్లు.		exclusive — they're not included	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0065	Now say it including your listener.		mana pērlu.	మన పేర్లు.		inclusive	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0066	Which possessives are identical to their pronouns?		āyana, āviḍa, āme.	ఆయన, ఆవిడ, ఆమె.			src::site known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002

S0075	"""How much is this?"" — then answer ""fifty rupees."""		idi enta? / yābhai rūpāyalu.	ఇది ఎంత? / యాభై రూపాయలు.			src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::002
S0076	"""Ten people."""		padi mandi.	పది మంది.		mandi is the counter for larger groups	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0077	"How much is ""fifty lakh"" in plain digits?"		5,000,000.	5,000,000.		A lakh is 100,000. · A crore is 10,000,000.	src::site known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002
S0078	How did today go?		ī rōju eṭla gaḍichindi?	ఈ రోజు ఎట్ల గడిచింది?		Listen for: rōju (day) and eṭla (how). Those two words are enough to identify it.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::question set::002
S0079	Are you well? [respectful]		bāgunnārā?	బాగున్నారా?		Listen for: bāgu- plus a rising -ā at the end. That final -ā is the yes/no question marker — the same one you met on the song page.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002
S0080	Have you eaten? [respectful]		tinnārā?	తిన్నారా?		This one is coming whether you like it or not. See the follow-ups section — it matters more than the day question does.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002
S0081	It was good. / It's good.		bāgundi	బాగుంది		"Why you can already say this: it's bāgā (""well,"" Lesson 3) fused with undi (""it is,"" Lesson 1). Telugu doesn't need a separate past tense here — context carries it."	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0082	Very good, aunty.		chālā bāgundi attayya	చాలా బాగుంది అత్తయ్య		"Why you can already say this: chālā (""very"") plus attayya — the address form of atta from Lesson 4, the word that does double duty for aunt and mother-in-law."	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0083	There was a lot of work at the office today.		ī rōju āphīsulō chālā pani undi	ఈ రోజు ఆఫీసులో చాలా పని ఉంది		"Why you can already say this: -lō is the ""in"" ending, and undi is Lesson 1's ""there is."" Literally: today office-in much work is. No verb conjugation anywhere."	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::past set::002
S0084	I'm a bit tired.		koncheṁ alisipōyānu	కొంచెం అలిసిపోయాను		"Learn this as a block. alisipōyānu is past tense with a first-person ending — Lesson 9 territory. You'll take it apart later; for now it's one word. konchaṁ (""a little"") is independently useful and softens almost anything."	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0085	Today was a bit hard.		ī rōju koncheṁ kaṣṭaṁ	ఈ రోజు కొంచెం కష్టం		Why you can already say this: zero copula from Lesson 1 — no verb at all. Today a-little difficulty. kaṣṭaṁ is a high-frequency word worth knowing.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::002
S0086	It was good — I did a lot of work.		bāgundi, chālā pani chēsānu	బాగుంది, చాలా పని చేసాను		"The direct version. chēsānu is ""I did"" — past tense with the first-person -nu ending you know from unnānu. Memorise it whole for now; Lesson 9 explains the shape."	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::past set::002
S0087	It was good — a lot of work got done.		bāgundi, chālā pani ayyindi	బాగుంది, చాలా పని అయ్యింది		"This is the one I'd actually use. See the note below: Telugu reaches for ""it got done"" far more readily than ""I did it,"" and in a family setting that framing lands better."	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::002
S0088	It was good — a good piece of work got finished.		bāgundi, oka manchi pani pūrti ayyindi	బాగుంది, ఒక మంచి పని పూర్తి అయ్యింది		Look at oka doing exactly its job. This is the deep-dive use: one newly introduced thing the listener doesn't know about yet. Drop it and the sentence goes vague. And manchi is the Lesson 3 “good” — the adjective form of manchidi.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::002
S0089	But there was a lot of traffic on the way.		kānī dārilō ṭrāphik chālā ekkuva undi	కానీ దారిలో ట్రాఫిక్ చాలా ఎక్కువ ఉంది		Only three new words. kāni (but), dāri (way, road), ekkuva (a lot, excessive). Everything else you have: -lō from āphīsulō, plus chālā and undi.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::past set::002
S0090	How are you? [respectful]		mīru eṭla unnāru?	మీరు ఎట్ల ఉన్నారు?		Why you can already say this: mīru from Lesson 1, unnāru is the same existence verb with the respectful ending. The only new piece is eṭla.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::past form::question set::002
S0091	Is everyone at home well? [respectful]		iṇṭlō antā bāgunnārā?	ఇంట్లో అంతా బాగున్నారా?		"A very Telugu thing to ask. Enquiring after the whole household rather than the individual is standard and lands as warm. intlō is illu (""house"") plus the -lō you used above."	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::question set::002
S0092	How did your day go? [respectful]		mī rōju eṭla gaḍichindi?	మీ రోజు ఎట్ల గడిచింది?		"Straight mirroring. Take her question and swap in mī (""your,"" Lesson 5). Reusing someone's own construction back at them is the cheapest fluency trick there is, and it always fits."	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::4-6 form::question set::002

S0099	Those people are students.		vāḷḷu vidyārthulu.	వాళ్ళు విద్యార్థులు.			src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0100	“She is a teacher.” Neutral statement of profession.		āme ṭīchar.	ఆమె టీచర్.		bare predicate; English “a” does not need translation.	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::002
S0101	“Give me one.” The noun is already understood. [respectful]		okaṭi ivvaṇḍi.	ఒకటి ఇవ్వండి.		independent okaṭi, not bare oka. · The item is understood, so independent okaṭi takes its place.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::request set::002
S0102	“I bought one book, not two.”		nēnu oka pustakaṁ konnānu, reṇḍu kādu.	నేను ఒక పుస్తకం కొన్నాను, రెండు కాదు.		exact count makes oka meaningful.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::negative set::003
S0103	“The book is on the table.” Both speakers know which book.		pustakaṁ balla mīda undi.	పుస్తకం బల్ల మీద ఉంది.		context can identify a bare noun; ā pustakaṁ if you need to point it out. · if both people know which book. A bare noun is not automatically indefinite.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::past set::003
S0104	one book		oka pustakaṁ	ఒక పుస్తకం		or “a book.” Oka modifies the noun that follows it.	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::003
S0105	Take a/one book. [respectful]		oka pustakaṁ tīsukōṇḍi	ఒక పుస్తకం తీసుకోండి		Context decides how strongly the count matters.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::request set::003
S0106	I bought a book / the book.		nēnu pustakaṁ konnānu	నేను పుస్తకం కొన్నాను		The point may simply be what you bought.	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::003
S0107	I bought a/one book.		nēnu oka pustakaṁ konnānu	నేను ఒక పుస్తకం కొన్నాను		One book is placed into the conversation, especially naturally if you will say more about it.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::003
S0108	She is a doctor.		āme ḍākṭar	ఆమె డాక్టర్		This is the normal classification pattern. English requires a; Telugu does not.	src::site known::100 len::1-3 set::003
S0109	She is a good doctor.		āme oka manchi ḍākṭar	ఆమె ఒక మంచి డాక్టర్		Oka presents her more deliberately as one member/example of that category. Bare āme manci ḍākṭar is also natural.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 set::003
S0110	That book is on the table.		ā pustakaṁ balla mīda undi	ఆ పుస్తకం బల్ల మీద ఉంది		For an identifiable object, a demonstrative such as ā often does the job—not oka.	src::site known::100 len::4-6 form::past set::003
S0111	Please wait ten minutes. [respectful]		padi nimiṣālu āgaṇḍi	పది నిమిషాలు ఆగండి		The quantity is presented directly.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::1-3 form::request set::003
S0112	Please wait about ten minutes. [respectful]		oka padi nimiṣālu āgaṇḍi	ఒక పది నిమిషాలు ఆగండి		In contemporary speech, oka can soften a following quantity into an estimate.	src::site reg::respectful known::100 len::4-6 form::request set::003

S2343	I am a programmer		nēnu prōgrāmaruni	నేను ప్రోగ్రామరుని		Loanword + Telugu tail, same pattern as ṭīcharu (teacher). Marker -ni for nēnu.	src::anki known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2344	This is my son		itanu nā koḍuku	ఇతను నా కొడుకు		New word: koḍuku = son. Uses itanu, the near form of atanu (a→i rule from Lesson 1) — this is that rule in real use.	src::anki known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2345	That is my wife		āme nā bhārya	ఆమె నా భార్య		"Pure review — same shape as #3, distant/neutral ""she."""	src::anki known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2346	She is my son's mom [informal]		āme vāḍi amma	ఆమె వాడి అమ్మ		"New mini-pattern: vāḍi = ""his,"" the possessive of vāḍu (informal ""he"") — colloquially you refer to your own son as vāḍu affectionately, so ""vāḍi amma"" = ""his mom."""	src::anki reg::informal known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2347	We are family		manaṁ oka kuṭumbaṁ	మనం ఒక కుటుంబం		"New word: kuṭumbaṁ = family. Inclusive ""we"" + oka for emphasis — nice callback to the oka lesson."	src::anki known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2348	They are our friends		vāḷḷu mā snēhitulu	వాళ్ళు మా స్నేహితులు		New word: mā = our (possessive, same family as nā/mī). Plural of snēhituḍu irregularly drops to snēhitulu, not a plain -lu add — a small exception worth noting.	src::anki known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2349	He is a boy		atanu abbāyi	అతను అబ్బాయి		"Pure review — identical shape to ""atanu vidyārthi."""	src::anki known::100 len::1-3 set::047
S2350	You are my son [informal]		nuvvu nā koḍukuvi	నువ్వు నా కొడుకువి		"Informal ""you"" (own child) + marker -vi. Reuses koḍuku from #4."	src::anki reg::informal known::100 len::1-3 set::047
